{"version":1,"title":"Aiden Platform Operating Model Validation Study","backing_conversation_id":"6a5a3636-5540-83ea-93d0-6afdd26042d7","widget_session_id":"580b8c6c-d97c-446a-a784-d8ab5fe59a7c","widget_state":{"status":"completed","is_static_preview":true,"report_message":{"id":"361e2ee5-4b0e-4b00-a9ff-ae557491e8e6","author":{"role":"assistant","name":null,"metadata":{"real_author":"tool:web"}},"create_time":1784297594.569703,"update_time":null,"content":{"content_type":"text","parts":["# Aiden Platform Operating Model Validation Study\n\n## Executive conclusion\n\nThe attached July 17 document should be treated as a proposal, not as an accepted architecture: it explicitly labels itself as “Discovery synthesis and proposed future architecture,” says that no implementation is authorized, and says it must not modify or expand EO-2026-013. It also proposes that any future console should remain a view over repository and Atlas state rather than a second source of truth. fileciteturn0file0\n\nThe strongest external conclusion is that the proposed direction is **mostly sound if it is made smaller, more explicit, and more Git-native than the current proposal implies**. The externally supported pattern is not “AI owns the work,” and also not “the chat transcript is the workflow.” The proven pattern is: **durable task state outside the conversation, repository-backed instructions and policies, resumable execution state in a workflow runtime when needed, pull-request-centered review, and separate approval controls for code, deployments, and privileged tool use.** GitHub Copilot cloud agent, GitHub Agentic Workflows, Claude Code GitHub Actions, OpenAI Codex safety controls, LangGraph, Temporal, and Prefect all externalize critical state beyond the chat itself, though they do so in different layers. citeturn28view0turn30view3turn27view14turn29view0turn27view17turn27view19turn27view18\n\nThe proposal is **well supported** in four places. First, keeping **GitHub as canonical engineering record** is consistent with how leading coding-agent systems anchor work in issues, branches, PRs, workflow files, repository instructions, and session-linked commits. Second, an **Atlas-like deterministic layer** is strongly supported by the need to compile pinned context, preserve audit trails, and validate work outside of model memory. Third, keeping **AI systems replaceable** is supported by GitHub’s multi-agent model support, MCP’s open integration pattern, and the trend toward repo-owned skills and agent profiles rather than vendor-owned hidden state. Fourth, a **gradual autonomy ladder** is much better supported than any attempt to jump directly to unattended architectural change or auto-merge. citeturn27view5turn27view6turn27view7turn26view9turn6view2turn29view0turn29view2\n\nThe proposal is **premature or risky** in five places. Calling the interface “Aiden OS” risks implying a new canonical platform layer when the evidence supports a **control plane or console**, not an operating system. A separate mutable application database is premature before the platform has proven that Git plus GitHub objects plus generated Atlas JSON are insufficient. A large custom interface is premature before one end-to-end protocol is validated in real work. Broad agent authority is not supported by current evidence. And any acceptance model that relies mainly on passing tests is too weak: recent research shows that passing functional tests is not a reliable proxy for overall code quality, security, or reproducibility. citeturn31view1turn31view3turn33view0turn33view1turn32view1\n\nThe best near-term move is therefore **not** to build a broad console or “platform OS.” It is to build the **smallest responsible control-surface stack** that proves three things in day-to-day engineering: that a durable task object improves human understanding; that handoff from conversation to repo work becomes traceable; and that agent-generated work can be accepted or rejected based on explicit evidence rather than conversational confidence. citeturn31view4turn31view5turn27view3turn29view2turn34view0\n\n## External precedent and design principles\n\n### Documented facts\n\nAcross the strongest primary sources, the winning pattern is to keep **state, permissions, and evidence external to the chat turn**. GitHub Copilot cloud agent can be started from an issue or prompt, but it persists state in a branch or PR plus session logs; assigning an issue always creates a PR, while prompt mode works on a branch by default for review and iteration before PR creation. GitHub also keeps a separate session layer with progress, token usage, logs, steering, archiving, share controls, and queryable session history. citeturn28view0turn28view1turn30view3turn30view0turn30view1turn30view2\n\nGitHub Agentic Workflows formalize the same idea in workflow form. The author writes a Markdown workflow with frontmatter specifying triggers, permissions, engine, and `safe-outputs`; the compiled workflow is read-only by default unless explicit permissions are granted, and write operations are constrained to validated safe outputs such as creating issues or PRs. Secrets are deliberately kept outside the agent runtime. citeturn23search0turn27view13turn27view14\n\nAnthropic pushes a similar split, but in a more general agent-framework language. Anthropic distinguishes **workflows** from **agents**, recommends using the simplest solution that works, and warns that extra framework layers can obscure prompts and responses and tempt teams into unnecessary complexity. Anthropic’s evaluations guidance also says that shipping agents without evals leads teams into reactive loops and that evaluation value compounds over the full lifecycle of an agent. citeturn31view0turn31view1turn31view4turn31view5\n\nOpenAI’s current Codex safety guidance reinforces the same design lesson from the control side. Codex is intended to be productive inside a bounded environment; sandboxing defines the technical boundary, approval policy determines when actions must stop for review, network access is allowlisted rather than open-ended, and the system can emit OpenTelemetry logs covering user prompts, approval decisions, tool execution, MCP usage, and network allow/deny events. For enterprise workspaces, Codex activity can also be surfaced through the Compliance Platform, which offers immutable append-only compliance logs. ChatGPT Work extends this pattern at the product layer by supporting long-running tasks that users can follow, redirect, and explicitly approve when important actions are needed. citeturn29view0turn29view1turn29view2turn29view4turn36view0turn27view11\n\nDurable workflow systems make the same architectural choice even more explicitly. Temporal is built around persisted workflow history so applications resume where they left off after failure. LangGraph persists thread state as checkpoints, separates checkpointed graph state from longer-lived stores, and uses interrupts so execution can stop indefinitely until approval or input arrives. Its human-in-the-loop middleware makes approvals first-class decisions such as approve, edit, and reject. Prefect similarly supports pausing or suspending a flow until a human provides input through the UI. citeturn27view19turn27view17turn27view15turn27view16turn27view18\n\n### Architectural analogies\n\nThe closest high-confidence analogy for Aiden is **not** “personal operating system” in the literal sense. It is a **repository-centered engineering control plane** with four layers: canonical code and docs in Git/GitHub; deterministic context compilation and validation in Atlas; execution in replaceable workspaces and bounded automations; and a thin human control surface that reads from those layers and writes back only through explicit task, PR, and approval mechanisms. That analogy fits GitHub Agentic Workflows, Copilot sessions, Codex approvals, LangGraph checkpoints, and durable execution systems much better than an “AI-native OS” framing does. citeturn27view14turn30view3turn29view0turn27view17turn27view19\n\nA second useful analogy is **supply-chain provenance**. SLSA provenance treats build evidence as structured, verifiable metadata describing who built an artifact, from what inputs, with what recipe, when, and with what invocation ID and completeness claims. That is not identical to task-state modeling, but it is the right mental model for acceptance evidence: an agent change should come with enough structured metadata that a reviewer can tell what was attempted, on which revision, using which context and inputs, and which evidence is complete versus missing. citeturn34view0\n\nA third useful analogy is **observability rather than memory**. OpenTelemetry distinguishes traces, metrics, logs, and contextual baggage. The relevant implication for Aiden is that a mature control surface should not try to “remember everything” as prose. It should expose task traces, state transitions, approval events, validation logs, and linked evidence records. citeturn35view0turn35view2\n\n### Inferred implications\n\nThe key design implication is that **AI conversations should be treated as workspaces, not as the system of record**. In the external systems above, the conversation can start work, steer work, or explain work, but critical state is persisted in more stable objects: issue/PR/session artifacts, workflow history, checkpoint stores, compiled workflows, or compliance logs. That makes the user’s problem statement — “important intent and authorization can remain trapped in AI conversations” — directly validated by precedent. citeturn28view0turn30view3turn29view2turn36view0turn27view17turn27view19\n\nA second implication is that **authorization must be orthogonal to lifecycle**. GitHub separates PR review requirements from environment deployment reviewers; Agentic Workflows separates repository permissions from safe outputs; OpenAI separates sandbox boundaries from approval policy; and LangGraph separates whatever the agent wants to do from whether a human approves, edits, or rejects the proposed tool call. So Aiden should never infer that “later lifecycle stage” means “higher agent authority.” citeturn27view8turn27view10turn27view14turn29view0turn27view16\n\nA third implication is that **current model quality does not justify broad unattended acceptance**. Research on AI-generated code continues to show substantial hidden failure modes: package hallucinations; non-reproducible dependencies; weak correlation between test-passing performance and code quality/security; benchmark contamination and inadequate tests; and persistent non-determinism even at low temperature. That evidence supports bounded draft generation and bounded maintenance far more strongly than autonomous architecture change, autonomous merge, or deployment authority. citeturn32view0turn32view1turn33view0turn33view3turn33view4turn33view6\n\n### Recommendations\n\nThe Aiden Platform should adopt three first principles.\n\n**Recommendation.** Make **task state durable and inspectable** outside the conversation. If a task cannot be resumed, redirected, or reviewed without reopening a specific chat, it is not yet in the operating model. This is the single most important validation outcome from the external study. citeturn30view0turn30view3turn27view17turn27view19\n\n**Recommendation.** Keep **canonical truth sparse and layered**. Put only stable, reviewable, high-value state in Git and GitHub; put resumable runtime state in a workflow engine only when you truly need timers, retries, or indefinite pauses; and treat generated context bundles as caches derived from canonical state, never as canonical state themselves. citeturn27view14turn27view17turn27view19turn27view18\n\n**Recommendation.** Define the human interface as a **control surface over evidence**, not as a dashboard over vibes. The interface should answer: what is the mission, what is active, what needs approval, what changed, what evidence exists, and what is the next valid action. That pattern is directly aligned with GitHub session views, ChatGPT Work supervision, Codex telemetry, and workflow-runtime visibility. citeturn30view3turn27view11turn29view2turn27view19\n\n## Comparison of external system patterns\n\nThe comparison below focuses on **how serious systems externalize state and approvals**, not on model branding.\n\n| System pattern | How task state is externalized | How approvals and safety work | Resumability and audit pattern | What Aiden should borrow |\n|---|---|---|---|---|\n| **GitHub Copilot cloud agent** | Starts from issues or prompts; issues create PRs; prompt mode starts on a branch; context is persisted in PRs, branches, session logs, and signed commits linked back to the session. citeturn28view0turn27view2 | Human review remains separate; Copilot’s own PR approval does not satisfy required PR approvals; workflows can be held behind “Approve and run workflows.” citeturn27view3turn27view4 | Sessions can be monitored, steered, stopped, archived, shared, and queried later. citeturn30view0turn30view1turn30view2 | PR-centered execution, session-linked audit, steerable branch-first work. |\n| **GitHub Agentic Workflows** | Workflow intent is durable in repo Markdown plus compiled lockfile; frontmatter holds trigger, permissions, engine, and safe outputs. citeturn23search0turn23search1 | Read-only by default; write actions must be declared in `safe-outputs`; secrets stay outside the agent runtime. citeturn27view14 | Standard GitHub Actions run history plus repository-managed workflow files. citeturn23search1 | Markdown protocols, compiled automation, explicit output allowlists. |\n| **Anthropic Claude Code and Claude Code Action** | Repo instructions live in `CLAUDE.md`; repository skills and workflow files externalize standards and automation configuration. citeturn6view3turn27view5 | GitHub app permissions are explicit; action docs emphasize limiting permissions and reviewing suggestions before merge. citeturn5view0turn6view3turn20search1 | GitHub runners and workflow logs provide visible execution history. citeturn5view0turn20search0 | Repo-owned guidance files and scoped automation inputs. |\n| **OpenAI Codex and ChatGPT Work** | Long-running work is observable as managed tasks; Codex ties actions to sandbox config, approval policy, workspace controls, and compliance logs rather than to hidden chat state alone. citeturn27view11turn29view0turn29view4turn36view0 | High-risk actions stop for review; approvals can be one-off or session-scoped; plugin/app controls can require action confirmation and preserve source-system permissions. citeturn29view0turn27view12 | OpenTelemetry and Compliance logs capture prompts, approvals, tool events, and network policy decisions. citeturn29view2turn36view0 | Explicit approval scopes, allowlisted network/tool boundaries, agent-native telemetry. |\n| **LangGraph** | Graph state is checkpointed; long-term data can be stored outside thread state in dedicated stores. citeturn27view17 | Interrupts pause proposed actions until human input arrives; humans can approve, edit, or reject tool calls. citeturn27view15turn27view16 | Resume after pause or error from persistent checkpoint. citeturn27view15turn27view17 | Distinguish task-state record from persisted runtime thread state. |\n| **Temporal** | Workflow history is durable and failure-resilient. citeturn27view19turn13search0 | Human approval gates are implemented through signals/updates into the workflow. citeturn13search0 | Workflows resume exactly where they left off after failure. citeturn27view19 | Add a real workflow engine only when Aiden truly needs durable multi-day runtime state. |\n| **Prefect** | Flow runs keep workflow progress in a dedicated orchestration layer. citeturn27view18 | Flows can pause or suspend until a user provides input in the UI. citeturn27view18 | Resumption is part of the platform, not improvised in chat. citeturn27view18 | A UI-visible approval queue once unattended runs exist. |\n\nThe common pattern across these systems is that **task state is split across several layers rather than forced into one giant artifact**. Stable intent, standards, and permissions live in repo files or product objects; execution state lives in session history or workflow runtime; and accept/reject decisions are visible in PRs, approval events, or workflow signals. That is the pattern Aiden should adopt. citeturn23search0turn30view3turn29view0turn27view17turn27view19\n\n## Recommended Aiden operating model\n\n### Recommended task-state model\n\n**Recommendation.** Aiden should use a **hybrid task-state model** with one canonical task object, one derived context package, and one evidence bundle. The task object should be durable, human-readable, and repo-owned. The context package should be Atlas-generated and disposable. The evidence bundle should aggregate verification results, linked artifacts, and approval history. This matches the durable-state patterns in GitHub, LangGraph, and supply-chain provenance systems. citeturn30view3turn27view17turn34view0\n\nA minimal Aiden task object should carry the fields below. The field list is a recommendation, but each item is externally justified by patterns already used in GitHub sessions, workflow systems, Codex controls, or provenance models. citeturn28view0turn30view3turn29view0turn34view0\n\n```yaml\nid: TASK-2026-####\nkind: engineering | research | review | maintenance | automation\ntitle:\nobjective:\nsuccess_criteria:\nowner:\ncreated_at:\nstate:\nlifecycle_protocol:\n\nrepository:\n  repo:\n  base_branch:\n  base_sha:\n  working_branch:\n  worktree_id:\n  included_paths: []\n  excluded_paths: []\n  revision_freshness_checked_at:\n\nscope:\n  summary:\n  explicit_in_scope: []\n  explicit_out_of_scope: []\n  prohibited_actions: []\n  assumptions: []\n  stale_assumption_checks: []\n\nauthority:\n  actor: human | atlas | agent\n  permission_level: observe | propose | prepare | modify_bounded | open_pr\n  tool_permissions: []\n  network_permissions: []\n  protected_paths: []\n  requires_human_before: []\n\ncontext_package:\n  package_id:\n  generated_by:\n  generated_at:\n  source_refs: []\n  omitted_refs: []\n  freshness_notes:\n\noutputs_expected:\n  repo_changes:\n  docs_changes:\n  evidence_artifacts:\n  review_summary:\n\nverification:\n  required_checks: []\n  required_manual_checks: []\n  acceptance_tests: []\n  static_analysis: []\n  architecture_impact_review: []\n  docs_sync_required: true\n\napprovals:\n  plan_approved_by:\n  privileged_action_approvals: []\n  merge_approved_by:\n  deploy_approved_by:\n\nattempts:\n  count:\n  retries_allowed:\n  latest_failure:\n  last_resumable_state:\n\naudit:\n  linked_issue:\n  linked_pr:\n  session_ids: []\n  workflow_run_ids: []\n  evidence_bundle_id:\n```\n\n**Recommendation.** Store the canonical task object in the repository, not in the chat. A plausible V1 path is a repo-owned directory such as `.aiden/tasks/` with stable YAML objects, plus `.aiden/context/` for Atlas-generated JSON packages and `.aiden/evidence/` for generated evidence manifests. Issues and PRs should link to the task object rather than replace it. This keeps canonical state reviewable in Git while still allowing GitHub-native discussion and execution. The arrangement is an inference from GitHub’s use of repo-managed instructions and workflows, not a documented standard. citeturn23search0turn27view5turn27view6turn27view7turn34view0\n\n### Where each kind of state belongs\n\n**Recommendation.** Put **code, architecture docs, protocol schemas, task schemas, agent profiles, skills, generated static console artifacts, and acceptance criteria** in Git because they benefit from review, history, branch semantics, and PR discipline. GitHub already uses repository files for workflow frontmatter, custom agent profiles, skills, and instructions; that is directly analogous to repo-owned Aiden task and protocol definitions. citeturn23search0turn27view5turn27view6turn27view7\n\n**Recommendation.** Put **intake discussion, backlog triage, linked execution conversations, and review commentary** in GitHub issues and PRs. External precedent is strongest here: issues are good for task entry and context capture, PRs are good for change proposals and review, and branch/PR linkage is the most legible path for human understanding. citeturn28view0turn27view3turn27view10\n\n**Recommendation.** Put **retries, timers, waiting-for-input, and resumable execution state** in a workflow runtime only when unattended or multi-day work is actually needed. GitHub Actions is often enough for short-lived repo automation; Temporal, LangGraph, or Prefect become relevant when you need durable pause/resume or signal-based approvals. Adding that layer before it is needed would be complexity without evidence. citeturn23search1turn27view17turn27view19turn27view18turn31view1\n\n**Recommendation.** Put **structured repository object indexes and context compilations** in Atlas-generated JSON. Those are derived views, closer to compiled artifacts than to canonical objects. They should be rebuildable from repository truth plus current GitHub state. citeturn23search0turn34view0\n\n**Recommendation.** Keep **AI conversations** for brainstorming, sense-making, and steering, but require durable extraction of anything that matters: objective, scope, approval, evidence requirements, or acceptance decision. GitHub’s own agent docs mirror this: Copilot issue assignment only sees comments present at assignment time, so follow-up information must move into the PR or another durable object. citeturn28view2turn30view3\n\n### Recommended workspace-handoff model\n\n**Documented fact.** A strong pattern in existing agent systems is that human discussion, execution branches, and review objects are linked but separate. GitHub lets users start from an issue, branch, prompt, session, or PR; OpenAI Work lets users follow progress and change direction; and GitHub sessions can later be queried and traced through commit-linked logs. citeturn28view0turn30view3turn27view11\n\n**Recommendation.** Aiden should formalize the handoff as:\n\n```mermaid\nflowchart LR\n    H[Human intent] --> T[Repo-owned task object]\n    T --> C[Atlas context package at pinned SHA]\n    C --> W[Selected workspace]\n    W --> B[Branch or worktree]\n    B --> V[Verification runs]\n    V --> P[Draft PR with evidence]\n    P --> R[Human review and approval]\n    R --> M[Merge or reject]\n    M --> U[Task state update]\n```\n\nThis flow is supported by GitHub’s issue/branch/PR/session model, by Codex’s bounded-action approval model, and by workflow systems that treat runtime pause/resume as a separate layer from canonical intent. citeturn28view0turn30view3turn29view0turn27view17turn27view19\n\n**Recommendation.** The workspace selector should be explicit, not magical. Aiden should choose a workspace based on task type, risk, and needed tools: discussion-heavy and research-heavy work can begin in chat-like environments; bounded code changes go to a coding workspace or branch; long-running unattended work goes to automation only if the task object already encodes replayable scope and approvals. That recommendation is an inference from Anthropic’s workflow-versus-agent distinction and GitHub’s separation between issue assignment, prompt sessions, and agentic workflows. citeturn31view0turn28view0turn23search1\n\n### Recommended human-approval model\n\n**Documented fact.** Mature systems separate approval types. GitHub has PR approvals, environment reviewers for deployments, and workflow-run approvals; LangGraph exposes approve, edit, and reject; OpenAI plugin/app controls can require action confirmation; and Codex distinguishes sandbox policy from approval policy. citeturn27view3turn27view4turn27view8turn27view16turn27view12turn29view0\n\n**Recommendation.** Aiden should define four approval gates, each with its own semantics.\n\n**Plan approval** authorizes the objective, in-scope work, and out-of-scope boundaries. It should exist before privileged execution begins. This is analogous to the decision to create or assign a task. citeturn28view0turn29view0\n\n**Privileged action approval** covers actions that cross a boundary: wide file writes, sensitive tool use, new network destinations, changes to protected paths, workflow modifications, or operations work. This directly follows the Codex sandbox/approval split and GitHub’s ability to restrict sensitive paths and workflows. citeturn29view0turn29view1turn24search0turn27view10\n\n**Merge approval** validates that the proposed code change is accepted into the canonical repo. It should require a human other than the agent itself and should never be inferred from “the agent says it is done.” GitHub explicitly prevents a Copilot-generated PR approval from counting toward required human approvals. citeturn27view3\n\n**Deployment approval** should stay separate from merge approval. GitHub’s environment reviewers show why: deployment is a distinct risk boundary, and self-review can be prevented. citeturn27view8turn7search3\n\n### Recommended agent-authority model\n\n**Recommendation.** The current evidence supports the autonomy ladder below.\n\n| Authority level | What is appropriate now | Why |\n|---|---|---|\n| **Observe** | Health reports, repository audits, diff summaries, dependency findings, research summaries. citeturn23search1turn27view11 | Strong precedent and low blast radius. |\n| **Propose** | Plans, architecture alternatives, task objects, review comments, acceptance criteria drafts. citeturn31view1turn31view4 | High value, easily human-gated. |\n| **Prepare** | Create branches, edit bounded files, run checks, assemble evidence, open draft PRs. citeturn28view0turn30view3 | Strong precedent in GitHub, Copilot, Claude Code, and Codex. |\n| **Execute bounded maintenance** | Very narrow chores such as formatting, generated-file refresh, low-risk docs sync, dependency report refresh, or lint/test repair under explicit path and output constraints. citeturn23search1turn24search0turn29view0 | Plausible only with path rules, evidence, and human review. |\n| **Autonomous merge or deploy** | **Not recommended for V1.** citeturn27view3turn27view8turn33view0turn32view1 | Evidence does not support broad trust; hidden failure modes remain large. |\n| **Autonomous architecture change** | **Not recommended.** citeturn31view1turn33view5turn33view6 | Simplicity, context mismatch, and non-determinism all argue against it. |\n\n**Recommendation.** The “merge” authority level in the attached proposal should be moved out of the normal ladder for now. Current precedent supports draft PR generation and bounded draft automation far more strongly than merge autonomy; GitHub’s own approval model reinforces that distinction. fileciteturn0file0 citeturn27view3turn27view8\n\n### Recommended verification and acceptance model\n\n**Documented fact.** The external evidence is unusually consistent here. Anthropic says evals must become part of the lifecycle, not an afterthought. GitHub says Copilot output needs the same thorough review as any other contribution. Recent research finds that passing functional tests does not reliably predict code quality or security, and another study finds that only 68.3% of AI-generated projects execute out of the box with specified dependencies. These are exactly the kinds of false-confidence failure modes the user called out. citeturn31view4turn26view2turn33view0turn33view1turn32view1\n\n**Recommendation.** Agent-generated changes should not be accepted without an **evidence bundle** containing, at minimum: pinned base SHA; changed paths; linked task object; required test or check results; at least one static-analysis or lint result where applicable; documentation sync evidence; an architecture-impact note explaining why existing abstractions or utilities were reused rather than duplicated; and explicit reviewer disposition. For binary or deployed artifacts, add provenance or attestation where possible. This recommendation is an inference from GitHub session linkage, Anthropic lifecycle eval guidance, SLSA provenance structure, and the code-quality research above. citeturn27view2turn31view4turn34view0turn33view0turn32view1\n\n**Recommendation.** Aiden should explicitly test for the failure modes the user named. Hidden redundancy and unnecessary abstraction should be checked through architecture-impact review and reuse checks. Quiet technical debt should be checked through lint/static-analysis findings, dependency review, and docs sync. False confidence from passing tests should be checked by requiring more than test green status for acceptance. Context loss and stale assumptions should be checked by pinning the base SHA and recording freshness checks in the task object. Insecure tool access should be checked through tool/network allowlists. Review overload should be reduced by forcing small bounded tasks and better evidence summaries. citeturn29view0turn29view1turn33view0turn32view1turn32view0turn33view6\n\n## Human control surface and smallest checkpoint\n\n### Recommended V1 control-surface architecture\n\n**Recommendation.** The best V1 is a **generated static console backed by repo-owned objects plus Atlas-generated JSON**, not a bespoke application with a mutable database. That recommendation is directly aligned with the attached proposal’s non-goal of making the console an independent source of truth and with the external pattern of repo-managed workflows, agent profiles, and instructions. fileciteturn0file0 citeturn23search0turn27view6turn27view7\n\nA practical V1 stack looks like this:\n\n- **Git** for canonical task objects, protocol definitions, capability registry entries, agent profiles, skills, and generated site source.\n- **GitHub Issues** for intake, visible work queues, and linked discussion.\n- **GitHub PRs** for every code-bearing change proposal and its review.\n- **GitHub Actions** for CI, evidence assembly, and narrow automations.\n- **Atlas-generated JSON** for compiled task-context packages, dependency maps, and control-surface views.\n- **Generated static site** for the human console.\n- **No separate application database in V1**, except ephemeral local cache if needed for performance.\n- **No general workflow engine in V1**, unless the first real unattended use case proves GitHub Actions plus repo state is insufficient. citeturn23search0turn23search1turn28view0turn27view10turn27view17turn27view19\n\n```mermaid\nflowchart TD\n    G[Git repository] --> A[Atlas compiler and validator]\n    I[GitHub issues] --> A\n    P[GitHub pull requests] --> A\n    C[CI run metadata] --> A\n\n    A --> J[Generated Atlas JSON]\n    J --> S[Static control surface]\n    J --> X[Task-scoped context packages]\n    X --> W[Replaceable workspaces]\n\n    W --> PR[Draft branch or PR]\n    PR --> A\n    S --> H[Human review and approval]\n    H --> I\n    H --> P\n```\n\nThis architecture keeps the control surface close to the truth sources, keeps Atlas deterministic, and delays any separate orchestration datastore until there is runtime evidence that it is needed. That is exactly the sort of “simplest architecture that works” pattern Anthropic recommends. citeturn31view1turn31view3\n\n### Human-facing interface priorities\n\n**Recommendation.** The interface should initially optimize for **orientation and approval**, not for full execution from the UI. The best-supported panels are: current mission; active tasks; approval queue; recent agent activity; PR and verification state; dependency/impact map; and “next valid action.” These are the things GitHub session views, ChatGPT Work supervision, and workflow UIs already make visible. citeturn30view3turn27view11turn27view18\n\n**Recommendation.** The attached report’s proposed screens — home, capture, research, development, review, automation, operations, learning, platform map — are directionally sensible, but V1 should not implement them all as full products. Start with **Home**, **Task detail**, **Approval queue**, **Review/evidence view**, and **Platform map**. Leave broader experiences such as learning and operations as links or stubs until the task model proves itself in real engineering work. That is an inference from the attached proposal plus external simplicity guidance. fileciteturn0file0 citeturn31view1\n\n### Smallest responsible implementation checkpoint\n\nThe smallest checkpoint that can produce real evidence before a large UI build is:\n\n**Define one repo-owned task object and one protocol.** Create a single durable task type for engineering checkpoints plus one protocol for proposal → approved task → draft PR → review → accepted or rejected. This proves whether the operating model actually clarifies work. citeturn23search0turn27view10turn31view4\n\n**Compile one Atlas context package at pinned SHA.** The package should include objective, base SHA, in-scope paths, out-of-scope paths, allowed actions, prohibited actions, and required verification. This proves whether “conversation to repository implementation” can be made explicit. citeturn29view0turn34view0\n\n**Generate one static task page and one home page.** The task page should show canonical fields, linked issue/PR/session, verification results, approval status, and next valid action. The home page should show active tasks and pending approvals. This proves whether a control surface improves human understanding without a custom app. citeturn30view3turn27view11\n\n**Run one real engineering task through it.** Use a low-risk bounded task that produces a draft PR and evidence bundle, but does not merge automatically. This proves whether the protocol reduces ambiguity and review burden in practice. citeturn28view0turn27view3turn27view4\n\nIf that checkpoint fails, the right move is not “add more interface.” It is to revise the task schema and protocol. If it succeeds, only then should Aiden consider connected execution, queued approvals, or a workflow runtime. citeturn31view1turn31view4\n\n## Risks, patterns to avoid, and unresolved decisions\n\n### Patterns Aiden should avoid\n\n**Avoid putting important intent, authorization, or completion state only in chat.** External precedent overwhelmingly treats chat as one interface among several, not as the canonical container for task state. citeturn28view0turn30view3turn27view17\n\n**Avoid building a second source of truth in the console.** The interface should read from repo and GitHub truth, not become a mutable parallel state layer whose drift must itself be managed. The attached proposal already senses this risk, and external systems avoid it by persisting canonical state in repo objects, workflow history, or approval records rather than in the dashboard itself. fileciteturn0file0 citeturn23search0turn27view19\n\n**Avoid equating green CI with acceptance.** Functional tests alone are too weak; code-quality, security, and reproducibility failures remain common even when tests pass. citeturn33view0turn32view1turn33view2\n\n**Avoid broad network or tool access without explicit allowlists and approval semantics.** Codex’s current safety guidance strongly supports allowlisting and staged approval rather than broad ambient access. citeturn29view0turn29view1turn27view12\n\n**Avoid over-investing in skills or custom agents before measurement.** Repo-owned skills and agent profiles are useful, but research suggests skill injection is often far less helpful than enthusiasm implies; 39 of 49 evaluated SWE skills in one 2026 benchmark produced zero pass-rate improvement. citeturn27view5turn27view6turn33view5\n\n**Avoid prematurely adopting a workflow engine as a default architecture.** Durable runtimes are excellent once pause/retry/resume are real needs, but they are not a substitute for getting the canonical task model right first. citeturn27view19turn27view18turn31view1\n\n### Risk register\n\n| Risk | Why it matters | Likelihood | Impact | Mitigation |\n|---|---|---:|---:|---|\n| Conversation-only state | Hidden intent and approvals become unreviewable and non-resumable. citeturn28view2turn30view3 | High | High | Force extraction of task, scope, approvals, and evidence into durable objects. |\n| Duplicate truth between console and repo | Human interface becomes another drift source. fileciteturn0file0 | Medium | High | Make console generated-first; no separate canonical DB in V1. |\n| Protocol bureaucracy | Teams stop using the model if task creation is heavier than the work itself. citeturn31view1 | Medium | High | Start with one task type and one protocol; optimize after measuring pain. |\n| False confidence from passing tests | Passing tests may hide defects, vulnerabilities, or design debt. citeturn33view0turn32view1 | High | High | Require evidence bundles with static analysis, docs sync, architecture-impact note, and reviewer sign-off. |\n| Hidden dependency/runtime gaps | Generated changes may work only in the agent’s environment. citeturn32view1 | Medium | High | Pin base SHA, require reproducibility checks, and capture environment assumptions. |\n| Over-permissioned agents | Tool/network/file access can exceed human intent. citeturn29view0turn29view1turn27view12 | Medium | High | Separate permission level from lifecycle stage; allowlist tools, paths, and domains. |\n| Review overload | Faster generation can raise human review burden faster than actual delivery improves. citeturn26view2turn31view4 | High | Medium | Force smaller tasks, stronger summaries, and draft-PR rather than auto-merge workflow. |\n| Context/package staleness | Agents can act on obsolete repo assumptions. citeturn32view6turn28view2 | High | Medium | Pin context to base SHA and add freshness checks before execution. |\n| Premature autonomy | Unattended changes wander into architecture, workflow, or security boundaries. citeturn27view3turn27view8turn33view6 | Medium | Very high | Stop at bounded maintenance plus draft PR until real reliability evidence exists. |\n| Vendor lock-in | Tool-specific memories and hidden context reduce replaceability. citeturn6view2turn27view5turn27view6 | Medium | Medium | Keep standards, task objects, and context packages repo-owned and vendor-neutral. |\n\n### Unresolved owner decisions\n\nThe study leaves several real owner choices open.\n\nThe first is **naming**. Externally, the safer and clearer term is **Aiden Platform Console** or **Aiden Control Surface** rather than **Aiden OS**. The “OS” name is defensible only as an analogy; it is weak as a literal architecture description because it implies a system-of-record layer the evidence does not support. citeturn31view1turn31view3\n\nThe second is **task canonicality**. Should the canonical task object live primarily in a repo file linked from an issue, or in a GitHub issue mirrored into a repo file? The external evidence favors repo-owned canonical schema with GitHub-linked discussion, but the owner should decide how much GitHub-native ergonomics matters relative to Git-first durability. citeturn23search0turn28view0\n\nThe third is **when to introduce a workflow engine**. The evidence supports waiting until Aiden truly needs durable pause/resume, retries, timed waits, and approval queues that GitHub Actions cannot comfortably express. That threshold should be a conscious decision, not assumed in V1. citeturn27view19turn27view18\n\nThe fourth is **what counts as protected scope**. The owner should define which files or directories always require explicit approval, which tasks can create draft PRs unattended, and which tasks must remain human-started and human-closed. GitHub path rules, PR rules, and deployment reviewers give good enforcement primitives, but the policy itself is an owner choice. citeturn24search0turn27view10turn27view8\n\nThe fifth is **which unattended maintenance activities are actually worth automating first**. The evidence supports low-risk reporting, consistency checks, and narrow maintenance much more strongly than broad refactoring. The owner should choose the first candidate tasks based on actual review pain, not on available automation capability. citeturn23search1turn31view4turn33view0\n\n## Source ledger\n\nThe ledger below lists the primary external sources that materially support this study. For documents that do not state a publication date, the date field is marked accordingly rather than guessed.\n\n| Exact title | Organization | Publication date | Canonical raw URL | Source type |
